View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Further research on zombie thought processes yielded interesting results. As we know from the previous problem, the nervous system of a zombie consists of n brains and m brain connectors joining some pairs of brains together. It was observed that the intellectual abilities of a zombie depend mainly on the topology of its nervous system. More precisely, we define the distance between two brains u and v (1 ≤ u, v ≤ n) as the minimum number of brain connectors used when transmitting a thought between these two brains. The brain latency of a zombie is defined to be the maximum distance between any two of its brains. Researchers conjecture that the brain latency is the crucial parameter which determines how smart a given zombie is. Help them test this conjecture by writing a program to compute brain latencies of nervous systems.

In this problem you may assume that any nervous system given in the input is valid, i.e., it satisfies conditions (1) and (2) from the easy version.

Input

The first line of the input contains two space-separated integers n and m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 100000) denoting the number of brains (which are conveniently numbered from 1 to n) and the number of brain connectors in the nervous system, respectively. In the next m lines, descriptions of brain connectors follow. Every connector is given as a pair of brains a b it connects (1 ≤ a, b ≤ n and a ≠ b).

Output

Print one number – the brain latency.

Examples

Input

4 3
1 2
1 3
1 4


Output

2

Input

5 4
1 2
2 3
3 4
3 5


Output

3

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a nervous system with `n` brains and `m` connectors (edges) between brains
2. The distance between two brains is the minimum number of connectors needed to transmit a thought between them
3. The brain latency is the maximum distance between any two brains in the system
4. We need to find this maximum distance (diameter of the graph)

## Key Insight

This is essentially asking for the **diameter of a tree** (or more generally, a graph). Since we're dealing with distances between nodes, and given that the input represents a valid nervous system, this forms a connected graph.

For any connected graph, the diameter is the longest shortest path between any two nodes.

## Approach

Since we're dealing with unweighted graphs (each connector has weight 1), we can use a two-pass BFS approach:

1. Start BFS from any node (say node 1) to find the farthest node from it
2. Start BFS from that farthest node to find the farthest node from it
3. The distance from the second BFS will be the diameter (brain latency)

This works because in a tree (or any connected graph), the longest shortest path will be found by expanding from one end to the other.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Parse input to build adjacency list representation
2. Run BFS from node 1 to find the farthest node
3. Run BFS from that farthest node to find the maximum distance
4. Return that maximum distance

## Implementation

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def bfs(start, n, adj):
    """Run BFS from start node and return (farthest_node, distance)"""
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    queue = deque([(start, 0)])
    visited[start] = True
    farthest_node = start
    max_distance = 0
    
    while queue:
        node, distance = queue.popleft()
        if distance > max_distance:
            max_distance = distance
            farthest_node = node
            
        for neighbor in adj[node]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                visited[neighbor] = True
                queue.append((neighbor, distance + 1))
    
    return farthest_node, max_distance

# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Build adjacency list
adj = defaultdict(list)
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    adj[a].append(b)
    adj[b].append(a)

# First BFS from node 1 to find the farthest node
farthest1, _ = bfs(1, n, adj)

# Second BFS from that farthest node to find the diameter
_, brain_latency = bfs(farthest1, n, adj)

print(brain_latency)
```

## Verification with Examples

**Example 1:** n=4, m=3, edges: (1,2), (1,3), (1,4)
- Graph is a star with center at 1
- Distance from any node to center is 1, distance between any two leaves is 2
- Diameter = 2 ✓

**Example 2:** n=5, m=4, edges: (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (3,5)
- Structure: 1-2-3-4, 3-5
- Longest path: from 4 to 5 (4→3→5) = distance 2, or from 1 to 4 (1→2→3→4) = distance 3
- Diameter = 3 ✓

The algorithm correctly identifies the diameter of the graph.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a nervous system with `n` brains and `m` brain connectors
2. The distance between two brains is the minimum number of connectors needed to transmit a thought between them
3. Brain latency is the maximum distance between any two brains
4. We need to find the brain latency (diameter of the graph)

This is essentially asking for the **diameter of a tree** (or more generally, the longest shortest path in a connected graph).

## Key Insights

1. The brain latency is the maximum distance between any two brains in the network
2. In a tree (which is what we have here), the diameter is the longest path between any two nodes
3. We can find the diameter by:
   - Running BFS/DFS from any node to find the farthest node
   - Running BFS/DFS from that farthest node to find the farthest node from it
   - The distance from the second farthest node is the diameter

## Algorithm Steps

1. Build the graph from the input edges
2. Start BFS from node 1 to find the farthest node
3. Run BFS from that farthest node to find the maximum distance (diameter)
4. Return this maximum distance

## Implementation

```python
from collections import deque

def bfs(graph, start, n):
    """Return the farthest node and its distance from start"""
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    distance = [-1] * (n + 1)
    queue = deque([start])
    visited[start] = True
    distance[start] = 0
    
    farthest_node = start
    max_dist = 0
    
    while queue:
        node = queue.popleft()
        if distance[node] > max_dist:
            max_dist = distance[node]
            farthest_node = node
            
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                visited[neighbor] = True
                distance[neighbor] = distance[node] + 1
                queue.append(neighbor)
    
    return farthest_node, max_dist

# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Build adjacency list
graph = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]

for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    graph[a].append(b)
    graph[b].append(a)

# Find the diameter of the tree
# Step 1: Start BFS from node 1 to find one end of the diameter
end1, _ = bfs(graph, 1, n)

# Step 2: Start BFS from end1 to find the other end and the diameter
_, brain_latency = bfs(graph, end1, n)

print(brain_latency)
```

## Explanation of the Solution

1. **Graph Construction**: We build an adjacency list representation of the graph since we have undirected edges.

2. **Two BFS Approach**: 
   - First BFS from any node (node 1) finds the farthest node from it
   - Second BFS from that farthest node finds the maximum distance, which is the diameter

3. **Why this works**: In a tree, the diameter is the longest path between any two nodes. By finding the farthest node from an arbitrary start, then finding the farthest node from that one, we get the longest path (diameter).

4. **Time Complexity**: O(n + m) - we do two BFS traversals
5. **Space Complexity**: O(n + m) - for storing the graph and BFS data structures

For the examples:
- Example 1: 4 nodes, all connected to node 1. The longest path is from 2→1→3 or 2→1→4, distance = 2
- Example 2: A linear chain 1-2-3-4 and 3-5. The longest path is from 1→2→3→5 or 4→3→5, distance = 3

The solution correctly handles both examples.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a nervous system with `n` brains and `m` brain connectors (edges)
2. The distance between two brains is the minimum number of connectors needed to transmit a thought between them
3. The brain latency is the maximum distance between any two brains
4. We need to find this maximum distance (diameter of the graph)

This is essentially asking for the **diameter of a tree** (or more generally, for any connected graph).

## Key Insight

The brain latency is the diameter of the graph - the longest shortest path between any two nodes.

For a tree (which this input represents), we can find the diameter using this algorithm:
1. Pick any node and find the farthest node from it
2. From that farthest node, find the farthest node again
3. The distance between these two nodes is the diameter

This works because in a tree, the diameter is the longest path, and this two-pass algorithm will find it efficiently.

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Build the graph
- Read n, m
- Build an adjacency list representation of the graph

### Step 2: Find the farthest node from an arbitrary starting point
- Use BFS from node 1 to find the node that is farthest from it
- This gives us one end of the diameter

### Step 3: Find the farthest node from the end found in step 2
- Use BFS from that node to find the farthest node
- The distance to this farthest node is the diameter

### Step 4: Return the diameter

## Implementation

```python
from collections import deque

def bfs(start, n, graph):
    """Perform BFS from start node and return (farthest_node, distance)"""
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    distance = [-1] * (n + 1)
    queue = deque([start])
    visited[start] = True
    distance[start] = 0
    
    farthest_node = start
    max_distance = 0
    
    while queue:
        node = queue.popleft()
        if distance[node] > max_distance:
            max_distance = distance[node]
            farthest_node = node
        
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                visited[neighbor] = True
                distance[neighbor] = distance[node] + 1
                queue.append(neighbor)
    
    return farthest_node, max_distance

# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Build adjacency list
graph = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]

for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    graph[a].append(b)
    graph[b].append(a)

# Step 1: Find farthest node from node 1
farthest1, _ = bfs(1, n, graph)

# Step 2: Find farthest node from farthest1
farthest2, diameter = bfs(farthest1, n, graph)

print(diameter)
```

## Verification with Examples

**Example 1:** n=4, m=3, edges: (1,2), (1,3), (1,4)
- Graph is a star with center 1
- From node 1: farthest is any of 2,3,4 (distance 1)
- From node 2: farthest is node 4 (path: 2→1→4, distance 2)
- Diameter = 2 ✓

**Example 2:** n=5, m=4, edges: (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (3,5)
- This forms a tree with 3 as center
- From node 1: farthest is node 4 or 5 (distance 2)
- From node 4: farthest is node 5 (path: 4→3→5, distance 2) OR from 4 to 1: 4→3→2→1 (distance 3)
- Actually: from node 1: 1→2→3→4 (distance 3), so farthest is 4 or 5
- From node 4: 4→3→2→1 (distance 3), or 4→3→5 (distance 2)
- The farthest from 4 is 1 with distance 3
- Diameter = 3 ✓

Wait, let me reconsider example 2:
- From node 1: path to 4 is 1→2→3→4 (distance 3)
- So farthest from 1 is node 4 (distance 3)
- From node 4: path to 1 is 4→3→2→1 (distance 3)
- Path from 4 to 5 is 4→3→5 (distance 2)
- So farthest from 4 is node 1 (distance 3)
- Diameter = 3 ✓

The algorithm works correctly.

    """Perform BFS from start node and return (farthest_node, distance)"""
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    distance = [-1] * (n + 1)
    queue = deque([start])
    visited[start] = True
    distance[start] = 0
    
    farthest_node = start
    max_distance = 0
    
    while queue:
        node = queue.popleft()
        if distance[node] > max_distance:
            max_distance = distance[node]
            farthest_node = node
        
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                visited[neighbor] = True
                distance[neighbor] = distance[node] + 1
                queue.append(neighbor)
    
    return farthest_node, max_distance

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a nervous system with `n` brains and `m` brain connectors
2. The distance between two brains is the minimum number of connectors needed to transmit a thought between them
3. The brain latency is the maximum distance between any two brains in the system
4. We need to find this maximum distance (diameter of the graph)

## Key Insights

1. This is essentially finding the **diameter** of an undirected graph
2. The diameter is the longest shortest path between any two nodes
3. For any connected graph, we can find the diameter by:
   - Pick any node and find its farthest node (this gives us one end of the diameter)
   - From that farthest node, find the farthest node again (this gives us the other end)
   - The distance between these two nodes is the diameter

## Algorithm Steps

1. Build an adjacency list representation of the graph
2. Pick any starting node (say node 1)
3. Run BFS from that node to find the farthest node
4. Run BFS from the farthest node found in step 3 to find the farthest node from it
5. The distance to this second farthest node is our answer

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse Input
- Read n (number of brains) and m (number of connectors)
- Build adjacency list for the graph

### Step 2: First BFS
- Start from node 1
- Find the farthest node from node 1 and record its distance

### Step 3: Second BFS
- Start from the farthest node found in step 2
- Find the farthest node from it and record its distance
- This distance is the brain latency

### Step 4: Return the result

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1**: 4 brains, edges: (1,2), (1,3), (1,4)
- All brains are connected to brain 1
- Distance from 1 to any other brain = 1
- So maximum distance = 2 (between any two non-adjacent brains)

Wait, let me reconsider: distance between 2 and 3 is 2 (2→1→3), so yes, maximum distance is 2.

**Example 2**: 5 brains, edges: (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (3,5)
- The graph is a tree: 1-2-3-4 and 3-5
- Distance from 1 to 4: 1→2→3→4 = 3
- Distance from 1 to 5: 1→2→3→5 = 3
- Distance from 4 to 5: 4→3→5 = 2
- Maximum distance is 3

## Implementation

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def bfs(start, n, graph):
    """Run BFS from start node and return (farthest_node, distance)"""
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    queue = deque([(start, 0)])
    visited[start] = True
    farthest_node = start
    max_dist = 0
    
    while queue:
        node, dist = queue.popleft()
        if dist > max_dist:
            max_dist = dist
            farthest_node = node
            
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                visited[neighbor] = True
                queue.append((neighbor, dist + 1))
    
    return farthest_node, max_dist

# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Build adjacency list
graph = defaultdict(list)

for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    graph[a].append(b)
    graph[b].append(a)

# Find the first farthest node from node 1
farthest1, _ = bfs(1, n, graph)

# Find the farthest node from that farthest node
farthest2, brain_latency = bfs(farthest1, n, graph)

print(brain_latency)
```

The algorithm works because in any connected graph, the diameter (longest shortest path) can be found by:
1. Starting from any node and finding the farthest node
2. Starting from that farthest node and finding the farthest node again
3. The distance obtained in step 2 is the diameter

Time complexity: O(n + m) since we run BFS twice
Space complexity: O(n + m) for the graph representation and BFS queue

from collections import defaultdict, deque

def bfs(start, n, graph):
    """Run BFS from start node and return (farthest_node, distance)"""
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    queue = deque([(start, 0)])
    visited[start] = True
    farthest_node = start
    max_dist = 0
    
    while queue:
        node, dist = queue.popleft()
        if dist > max_dist:
            max_dist = dist
            farthest_node = node
            
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                visited[neighbor] = True
                queue.append((neighbor, dist + 1))
    
    return farthest_node, max_dist

# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Build adjacency list
graph = defaultdict(list)

for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    graph[a].append(b)
    graph[b].append(a)

# Find the first farthest node from node 1
farthest1, _ = bfs(1, n, graph)

# Find the farthest node from that farthest node
farthest2, brain_latency = bfs(farthest1, n, graph)

print(brain_latency)

Further research on zombie thought processes yielded interesting results. As we know from the previous problem, the nervous system of a zombie consists of n brains and m brain connectors joining some pairs of brains together. It was observed that the intellectual abilities of a zombie depend mainly on the topology of its nervous system. More precisely, we define the distance between two brains u and v (1 ≤ u, v ≤ n) as the minimum number of brain connectors used when transmitting a thought between these two brains. The brain latency of a zombie is defined to be the maximum distance between any two of its brains. Researchers conjecture that the brain latency is the crucial parameter which determines how smart a given zombie is. Help them test this conjecture by writing a program to compute brain latencies of nervous systems.

In this problem you may assume that any nervous system given in the input is valid, i.e., it satisfies conditions (1) and (2) from the easy version.

Input

The first line of the input contains two space-separated integers n and m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 100000) denoting the number of brains (which are conveniently numbered from 1 to n) and the number of brain connectors in the nervous system, respectively. In the next m lines, descriptions of brain connectors follow. Every connector is given as a pair of brains a b it connects (1 ≤ a, b ≤ n and a ≠ b).

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a nervous system with `n` brains and `m` brain connectors (edges)
2. The distance between two brains is the minimum number of connectors needed to transmit a thought between them
3. The brain latency is the maximum distance between any two brains in the system
4. We need to find the brain latency

This is essentially asking for the **diameter of the graph** - the longest shortest path between any two vertices.

## Key Insights

- The brain latency is the diameter of the graph (longest shortest path)
- For any connected graph, the diameter can be found by:
  1. Pick any vertex and find the farthest vertex from it
  2. From that farthest vertex, find the farthest vertex again
  3. The distance between these two vertices is the diameter

## Algorithm Steps

1. Build the graph from the input edges
2. Use BFS from an arbitrary starting vertex (say vertex 1) to find the farthest vertex
3. Use BFS from that farthest vertex to find the maximum distance (diameter)
4. Return this maximum distance

## Implementation

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def bfs(start, graph, n):
    """Perform BFS from start vertex and return (farthest_distance, farthest_vertex)"""
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    distances = [-1] * (n + 1)
    queue = deque([start])
    visited[start] = True
    distances[start] = 0
    farthest_vertex = start
    max_dist = 0
    
    while queue:
        current = queue.popleft()
        current_dist = distances[current]
        
        if current_dist > max_dist:
            max_dist = current_dist
            farthest_vertex = current
            
        for neighbor in graph[current]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                visited[neighbor] = True
                distances[neighbor] = current_dist + 1
                queue.append(neighbor)
    
    return max_dist, farthest_vertex

# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Build adjacency list
graph = defaultdict(list)

for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    graph[a].append(b)
    graph[b].append(a)

# Find diameter of the graph
# Step 1: BFS from vertex 1 to find one end of the diameter
first_max_dist, first_farthest = bfs(1, graph, n)

# Step 2: BFS from the farthest vertex found in step 1 to find the actual diameter
final_max_dist, _ = bfs(first_farthest, graph, n)

print(final_max_dist)
```

## Trace through examples:

**Example 1**: n=4, m=3, edges: (1,2), (1,3), (1,4)
- Graph: 1 connected to 2,3,4; 2,3,4 have no other connections
- BFS from vertex 1: distances are [0,1,1,1], farthest vertex is any of 2,3,4 with distance 1
- BFS from vertex 2: distances are [1,0,2,2], farthest vertex is 3 or 4 with distance 2
- Output: 2 ✓

**Example 2**: n=5, m=4, edges: (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (3,5)
- Graph forms a path: 1-2-3-4 and 1-2-3-5
- BFS from vertex 1: reaches distance 3 to vertices 4 and 5
- BFS from vertex 4: reaches distance 3 to vertex 5
- Output: 3 ✓

The algorithm correctly identifies the diameter in both cases.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a nervous system with `n` brains and `m` connectors (edges) between brains
2. The distance between two brains is the minimum number of connectors needed to transmit a thought between them
3. Brain latency is the maximum distance between any two brains in the system
4. We need to find the brain latency (diameter of the graph)

This is essentially asking for the **diameter of a tree** or connected graph - the longest shortest path between any two nodes.

## Key Insights

1. The problem describes a connected graph (since it's a valid nervous system)
2. The brain latency is the diameter of the graph - the longest shortest path between any two vertices
3. For a tree, the diameter can be found by:
   - Pick any node, find the farthest node from it
   - From that farthest node, find the farthest node again
   - The distance between these two nodes is the diameter

## Algorithm Steps

1. Build the graph from input edges
2. Use BFS/DFS from an arbitrary node (e.g., node 1) to find the farthest node
3. Use BFS/DFS from that farthest node to find the farthest node from it
4. The distance found in step 3 is the brain latency (diameter)

## Implementation

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def bfs(start, graph, n):
    """Perform BFS from start node and return the farthest node and its distance"""
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    queue = deque([(start, 0)])
    visited[start] = True
    farthest_node = start
    max_distance = 0
    
    while queue:
        node, distance = queue.popleft()
        if distance > max_distance:
            max_distance = distance
            farthest_node = node
        
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                visited[neighbor] = True
                queue.append((neighbor, distance + 1))
    
    return farthest_node, max_distance

# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Build graph (adjacency list)
graph = defaultdict(list)

for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    graph[a].append(b)
    graph[b].append(a)

# Find the diameter of the graph
# Step 1: Start from node 1, find the farthest node
farthest1, _ = bfs(1, graph, n)

# Step 2: Start from that farthest node, find the farthest node from it
farthest2, brain_latency = bfs(farthest1, graph, n)

print(brain_latency)
```

## Explanation of the Algorithm

1. **Graph Construction**: We build an undirected graph using adjacency lists
2. **BFS from node 1**: We find the node that is farthest from node 1 (this works because in any connected graph, the farthest node from any starting point will be one of the endpoints of the diameter)
3. **BFS from the farthest node**: We find the node that is farthest from the node found in step 2. The distance between these two nodes is the diameter (brain latency)

## Why This Works

In any connected graph, the diameter is the longest shortest path between any two nodes. The algorithm works because:
- The first BFS finds one endpoint of the diameter
- The second BFS finds the other endpoint of the diameter
- The distance between these endpoints is the diameter

## Time Complexity
- O(n + m) - each BFS visits each node and edge once
- This is efficient for the given constraints (n, m ≤ 100,000)

The solution handles both provided examples correctly:
- Example 1: A star graph with center 1 connected to 2,3,4. Diameter is 2 (distance between any two leaves)
- Example 2: A path 1-2-3-4 with 3-5. The diameter is 3 (distance between 1 and 5)

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a nervous system with `n` brains and `m` brain connectors (edges)
2. The distance between two brains is the minimum number of connectors needed to transmit a thought between them
3. Brain latency is the maximum distance between any two brains in the system
4. We need to find this maximum distance (diameter of the graph)

This is essentially finding the **diameter of a tree** (or more generally, the maximum distance between any two nodes in a connected graph).

## Key Insights

1. The nervous system forms a connected graph (given in the problem)
2. In a tree, the diameter can be found by:
   - Pick any node, find the farthest node from it
   - From that farthest node, find the farthest node again
   - The distance between these two nodes is the diameter

3. Since the problem states the input is valid (connected), we don't need to worry about disconnected components.

## Algorithm Steps

1. Build an adjacency list representation of the graph
2. From node 1, perform BFS to find the farthest node (let's call it u)
3. From node u, perform BFS to find the farthest node (let's call it v)
4. The distance from u to v is the brain latency (diameter)

## Why this works

In any connected graph, the diameter (longest shortest path) can be found by:
- Starting from any node, find the farthest node
- Starting from that farthest node, find the farthest node again
- The distance between these two nodes is the diameter

This works because in a connected graph, there's always a path between any two nodes, and the longest path must pass through the "center" of the graph.

## Implementation

```python
from collections import deque

def bfs(start, n, graph):
    """Perform BFS from start node and return (farthest_node, distance)"""
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    distance = [-1] * (n + 1)
    queue = deque([start])
    visited[start] = True
    distance[start] = 0
    
    farthest_node = start
    max_dist = 0
    
    while queue:
        node = queue.popleft()
        current_dist = distance[node]
        
        if current_dist > max_dist:
            max_dist = current_dist
            farthest_node = node
            
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                visited[neighbor] = True
                distance[neighbor] = current_dist + 1
                queue.append(neighbor)
    
    return farthest_node, max_dist

# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Build graph (adjacency list)
graph = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]

for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    graph[a].append(b)
    graph[b].append(a)

# Find diameter of the graph
# Step 1: From node 1, find the farthest node
u, _ = bfs(1, n, graph)

# Step 2: From node u, find the farthest node
v, brain_latency = bfs(u, n, graph)

print(brain_latency)
```

## Verification with examples

**Example 1:** n=4, m=3, edges: (1,2), (1,3), (1,4)
- This is a star graph with center at 1
- Distance from 1 to any other node is 1
- Farthest from 1: any of 2,3,4 (distance 1)
- From any leaf, say 2: distances are 1 to 1, 2 to 3, 2 to 4
- Maximum distance: 2 ✓

**Example 2:** n=5, m=4, edges: (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (3,5)
- This is a path: 1-2-3-4 and 3-5
- The longest path is from 1 to 4 or 1 to 5, which is 3 steps
- Distance from 1 to 4: 1→2→3→4 = 3 steps ✓

The algorithm correctly finds the brain latency (diameter) of the nervous system.

2. In an undirected tree (which this appears to be based on the problem context), the diameter can be found by:
   - Pick any node and find the farthest node from it
   - From that farthest node, find the farthest node again
   - The distance between these two nodes is the diameter

3. This works because in a tree, the longest path (diameter) will always go from one leaf to another leaf.

## Algorithm Steps

1. Build the graph from the input edges
2. Pick node 1 (or any node) and run BFS/DFS to find the farthest node from it
3. From that farthest node, run BFS/DFS again to find the farthest node from it
4. The distance found in step 3 is the brain latency

## Implementation

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def bfs(start, graph, n):
    """Run BFS from start node and return (farthest_node, distance)"""
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
    queue = deque([start])
    visited[start] = True
    dist[start] = 0
    
    farthest_node = start
    max_distance = 0
    
    while queue:
        node = queue.popleft()
        if dist[node] > max_distance:
            max_distance = dist[node]
            farthest_node = node
            
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                visited[neighbor] = True
                dist[neighbor] = dist[node] + 1
                queue.append(neighbor)
    
    return farthest_node, max_distance

# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Build graph
graph = defaultdict(list)
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    graph[a].append(b)
    graph[b].append(a)

# Step 2: Find farthest node from that farthest node

print(brain_latency)
```

**Example 1:** n=4, m=3, edges: (1,2), (1,3), (1,4)
- Graph: 1 connected to 2,3,4
- From node 1: farthest is any of 2,3,4 with distance 1
- From node 2: farthest is 3 or 4 with distance 2
- Brain latency = 2 ✓

**Example 2:** n=5, m=4, edges: (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (3,5)
- Graph: 1-2-3-4 and 3-5

- From node 4: farthest is 5 with distance 2 (4→3→5) or distance 2 (4→3→2→1)

The algorithm works correctly for both examples.

Submitted Code

View Extracted Code 
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def bfs(start, graph, n):
    """Run BFS from start node and return (farthest_node, distance)"""
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
    queue = deque([start])
    visited[start] = True
    dist[start] = 0
    
    farthest_node = start
    max_distance = 0
    
    while queue:
        node = queue.popleft()
        if dist[node] > max_distance:
            max_distance = dist[node]
            farthest_node = node
            
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                visited[neighbor] = True
                dist[neighbor] = dist[node] + 1
                queue.append(neighbor)
    
    return farthest_node, max_distance

# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Build graph
graph = defaultdict(list)
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    graph[a].append(b)
    graph[b].append(a)

# Find the diameter of the tree
# Step 1: Find farthest node from node 1
farthest1, _ = bfs(1, graph, n)

# Step 2: Find farthest node from that farthest node
farthest2, brain_latency = bfs(farthest1, graph, n)

print(brain_latency)

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You all know that the Library of Bookland is the largest library in the world. There are dozens of thousands of books in the library.

Some long and uninteresting story was removed...

The alphabet of Bookland is so large that its letters are denoted by positive integers. Each letter can be small or large, the large version of a letter x is denoted by x'. BSCII encoding, which is used everywhere in Bookland, is made in that way so that large letters are presented in the order of the numbers they are denoted by, and small letters are presented in the order of the numbers they are denoted by, but all large letters are before all small letters. For example, the following conditions hold: 2 < 3, 2' < 3', 3' < 2.

A word x1, x2, ..., xa is not lexicographically greater than y1, y2, ..., yb if one of the two following conditions holds: 

  * a ≤ b and x1 = y1, ..., xa = ya, i.e. the first word is the prefix of the second word; 
  * there is a position 1 ≤ j ≤ min(a, b), such that x1 = y1, ..., xj - 1 = yj - 1 and xj < yj, i.e. at the first position where the words differ the first word has a smaller letter than the second word has. 



For example, the word "3' 7 5" is before the word "2 4' 6" in lexicographical order. It is said that sequence of words is in lexicographical order if each word is not lexicographically greater than the next word in the sequence.

Denis has a sequence of words consisting of small letters only. He wants to change some letters to large (let's call this process a capitalization) in such a way that the sequence of words is in lexicographical order. However, he soon realized that for some reason he can't change a single letter in a single word. He only can choose a letter and change all of its occurrences in all words to large letters. He can perform this operation any number of times with arbitrary letters of Bookland's alphabet.

Help Denis to choose which letters he needs to capitalize (make large) in order to make the sequence of words lexicographically ordered, or determine that it is impossible.

Note that some words can be equal.

Input

The first line contains two integers n and m (2 ≤ n ≤ 100 000, 1 ≤ m ≤ 100 000) — the number of words and the number of letters in Bookland's alphabet, respectively. The letters of Bookland's alphabet are denoted by integers from 1 to m.

Each of the next n lines contains a description of one word in format li, si, 1, si, 2, ..., si, li (1 ≤ li ≤ 100 000, 1 ≤ si, j ≤ m), where li is the length of the word, and si, j is the sequence of letters in the word. The words are given in the order Denis has them in the sequence.

It is guaranteed that the total length of all words is not greater than 100 000.

Output

In the first line print "Yes" (without quotes), if it is possible to capitalize some set of letters in such a way that the sequence of words becomes lexicographically ordered. Otherwise, print "No" (without quotes).

If the required is possible, in the second line print k — the number of letters Denis has to capitalize (make large), and in the third line print k distinct integers — these letters. Note that you don't need to minimize the value k.

You can print the letters in any order. If there are multiple answers, print any of them.

Examples

Input

4 3
1 2
1 1
3 1 3 2
2 1 1


Output

Yes
2
2 3 

Input

6 5
2 1 2
2 1 2
3 1 2 3
2 1 5
2 4 4
2 4 4


Output

Yes
0


Input

4 3
4 3 2 2 1
3 1 1 3
3 2 3 3
2 3 1


Output

No

Note

In the first example after Denis makes letters 2 and 3 large, the sequence looks like the following:

  * 2'
  * 1
  * 1 3' 2'
  * 1 1



The condition 2' < 1 holds, so the first word is not lexicographically larger than the second word. The second word is the prefix of the third word, so the are in lexicographical order. As the first letters of the third and the fourth words are the same, and 3' < 1, then the third word is not lexicographically larger than the fourth word.

In the second example the words are in lexicographical order from the beginning, so Denis can do nothing.

In the third example there is no set of letters such that if Denis capitalizes them, the sequence becomes lexicographically ordered.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a sequence of words made of small letters (positive integers)
2. We can capitalize entire letters (make them large) - this affects all words
3. Large letters come before small letters in the ordering: 2' < 3' < 4' < ... and 2' < 1 (since all large letters come before all small ones)
4. We need to find which letters to capitalize so that the sequence is lexicographically ordered
5. If impossible, output "No"

## Key Insights

1. The ordering rule: large letters are always smaller than small letters, and among large letters, smaller numbers come first
2. For two consecutive words to be in order, either:
   - The first word is a prefix of the second, OR
   - At the first position where they differ, the first word has a smaller letter (considering capitalization)
3. When we capitalize a letter, it becomes large and is smaller than all small versions of any letter
4. The capitalization decision is global - we decide for each letter whether to make it large or small

## Algorithm

1. For each adjacent pair of words, check if they're in order with any possible capitalization
2. We can think of this as a constraint satisfaction problem:
   - For each position where two words differ, we need to ensure the letter in the first word is "smaller" than the letter in the second word
   - We can represent the relationship: if we have letter x in word i and letter y in word j at the same position, we need x' < y or x < y
   - Since large letters are smaller than small ones, we need to determine the capitalization status

3. We can model this as a directed graph where:
   - Each letter is a node
   - For each conflicting pair, we add edges based on the required ordering
   - If we have a cycle in the constraints, it's impossible

4. Actually, let me think simpler:
   - For each adjacent pair of words, we find the first position where they differ
   - If at position j, word i has letter a and word i+1 has letter b:
     - If a is capitalized and b is not: a' < b (which is true since a' is large and b is small)
     - If a is not capitalized and b is capitalized: a > b' (which is false since a is small and b' is large)
     - If both are not capitalized: a > b (which is false if a > b)
     - If both are capitalized: a' < b' (which is true if a < b)

   - So the valid cases are:
     - a < b (both not capitalized) 
     - a < b (a capitalized, b not) - this gives a' < b, which is true since a' < b
     - a' < b' (both capitalized) - this is true if a < b
     - a' < b (a capitalized, b not) - true
     - a < b' (a not capitalized, b capitalized) - true since a < b'
     - a' < b' (both capitalized) - true if a < b

   - Actually, let me re-express:
     - If a > b: we must have a' < b (a capitalized) OR a' < b' (both capitalized) OR a < b' (a not capitalized, b capitalized)
     - But a < b' is always true since any large letter < any small letter
     - a' < b' is true if a < b
     - a' < b is true if a' < b (a is large, b is small)

   - So if a > b, we can fix it by capitalizing a or by capitalizing b
   - If a < b, we can fix it by not capitalizing either, or by capitalizing a (which gives a' < b), or by capitalizing b (which gives a < b').

   - The constraint is: if a > b, then either a must be capitalized OR b must be capitalized
   - If a < b, no constraint (we can make either choice)

5. This gives us a constraint: for each conflicting position where word_i has a letter > letter in word_{i+1}, we need at least one of them to be capitalized.

6. We can build a graph and use union-find or topological sort to check for contradictions.

Let me implement a solution:

```python
        letters = line[1:]
        words.append(letters)

    # if words[i][j] > words[i+1][j]: then we need words[i][j] to be capitalized OR words[i+1][j] to be capitalized

    # We can represent each letter by whether it's capitalized (true/false)

    # But this might lead to inconsistencies.
    
    # Instead, we can build a constraint graph and check for cycles:
    # Create a graph where each node is a letter
    # For each constraint "a OR b", add edges to represent dependencies
    
    # Actually, let's use a simpler approach:
    # For each constraint "a OR b", we can just store it. We need to check if there's a valid assignment.
    # We can try to build the set of letters that must be capitalized.
    
    # A better approach: use a union-find style for constraints, but it's 2-SAT.
    
    # Since the constraints are OR constraints, we can process them in order and use propagation.
    
    # Initialize: no letters are capitalized
    capitalized = [False] * (m + 1)  # 1-indexed
    
    # For each adjacent pair of words
    valid = True
    constraints = []  # list of (a, b) meaning a OR b must hold
    
    for i in range(n - 1):
        word1 = words[i]
        word2 = words[i + 1]
        
        min_len = min(len(word1), len(word2))
        found_diff = False
        
        for j in range(min_len):
            if word1[j] != word2[j]:
                found_diff = True
                if word1[j] > word2[j]:
                    # We need word1[j] to be capitalized OR word2[j] to be capitalized
                    constraints.append((word1[j], word2[j]))
                # If word1[j] < word2[j], no constraint needed
                break
        
        # If no difference in the common prefix, and word1 is longer, then word1 is not <= word2
        if not found_diff:
            if len(word1) > len(word2):
                # word1 is longer than word2 and they have the same prefix - word1 > word2
                valid = False
                break
    
    if not valid:
        print("No")
        return
    
    # Check if the constraints are satisfiable
    # We have a set of OR constraints: for each (a,b), at least one of a or b must be capitalized
    
    # We can use a simple greedy approach: assign capitalization to satisfy constraints
    # For each constraint (a,b), if neither a nor b is capitalized, then we must make one of them capitalized
    
    # We can try to assign capitalizations greedily
    # But there might be cycles - for example, constraint (1,2) and (2,3) and (3,1)
    
    # The OR constraints form a graph where we need to assign True/False to nodes such that each edge has at least one True.
    # This is equivalent to finding a vertex cover in a bipartite graph? Not exactly.
    
    # Actually, any OR constraint (a OR b) can be satisfied if we have at least one of them.
    # The constraints are not necessarily connected.
    
    # We can try to build the dependency graph and check for cycles, but it's complex.
    
    # Simple approach: try to satisfy constraints greedily and check for contradictions
    
    # Create a graph of constraints
    from collections import defaultdict
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    in_constraint = [0] * (m + 1)
    
    # Each constraint (a,b) means a or b must be true
    # We can build this as a graph and use a simple assignment
    
    # Let's try a simple greedy: for each constraint, if neither a nor b is marked, we mark one of them
    # But which one? We can just mark a, and if it causes problems later, we adjust.
    
    # Actually, there's a simpler way: we can just try to assign capitalizations such that all constraints are satisfied
    # and see if there's a contradiction in the assignment.
    
    # Since the total number of constraints might be large, we need a more efficient method.
    
    # But note: the constraint is OR, so we just need to ensure at least one is true.
    # We can process constraints and propagate the requirement.
    
    # Use a set of letters that must be capitalized
    must_capitalize = set()
    
    # Process each constraint
    for a, b in constraints:
        if a not in must_capitalize and b not in must_capitalize:
            # We need to capitalize at least one of a or b
            # We can choose either. Let's choose a (arbitrary)
            must_capitalize.add(a)
    
    # Now check if all constraints are satisfied
    valid_assignment = True
    for a, b in constraints:
        if (a not in must_capitalize) and (b not in must_capitalize):
            valid_assignment = False
            break
    
    if valid_assignment:
        print("Yes")
        print(len(must_capitalize))
        if must_capitalize:
            print(*sorted(must_capitalize))
        else:
            print()
    else:
        print("No")

solve()
```

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
words = []
for _ in range(n):
    line = list(map(int, input().split()))
    length = line[0]
    letters = line[1:]
    words.append(letters)

# Check for prefix violations
for i in range(n - 1):
    word1 = words[i]
    word2 = words[i + 1]
    min_len = min(len(word1), len(word2))
    
    # Check if word1 is a prefix of word2
    valid = True
    for j in range(min_len):
        if word1[j] != word2[j]:
            valid = False
            break
    
    if valid and len(word1) > len(word2):
        # word1 is longer and is a prefix of word2 - this violates lexicographic order
        print("No")
        exit()

# Build constraints: for each position where they differ and word1[j] > word2[j], we need either word1[j] or word2[j] to be capitalized
constraints = []

for i in range(n - 1):
    word1 = words[i]
    word2 = words[i + 1]
    min_len = min(len(word1), len(word2))
    
    for j in range(min_len):
        if word1[j] != word2[j]:
            if word1[j] > word2[j]:
                constraints.append((word1[j], word2[j]))
            break

# For each constraint (a, b), at least one of a or b must be capitalized
# We can always satisfy this by choosing one of them
capitals = set()

for a, b in constraints:
    if a not in capitals and b not in capitals:
        # Choose a (arbitrary)
        capitals.add(a)

print("Yes")
print(len(capitals))
if capitals:
    print(*sorted(capitals))
else:
    print()
```

Word3: 3 letters: [2,3,3] -> [2,3',3']
Word4: 2 letters: [3,1] -> [3,1]

Compare word1 and word2:
- Position 0: 3' vs 1 -> 3' < 1? Yes, because large letter < small letter. So word1 < word2.

Compare word2 and word3:
- Position 0: 1 vs 2 -> 1 < 2 -> word2 < word3.

Compare word3 and word4:
- Position 0: 2 vs 3 -> 2 < 3 -> word3 < word4.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())
words = []
for _ in range(n):
    line = list(map(int, input().split()))
    length = line[0]
    letters = line[1:]
    words.append(letters)

# Check for prefix violations: if word i is a prefix of word i+1 and word i is longer, it's invalid
for i in range(n - 1):
    word1 = words[i]
    word2 = words[i + 1]
    min_len = min(len(word1), len(word2))
    
    # Check if word1 is a prefix of word2
    is_prefix = True
    for j in range(min_len):
        if word1[j] != word2[j]:
            is_prefix = False
            break
    
    # If word1 is a prefix and len(word1) > len(word2), then word1 > word2, invalid
    if is_prefix and len(word1) > len(word2):
        print("No")
        exit()

# Build constraints: for each differing position where word1[j] > word2[j], we need either word1[j] or word2[j] to be capitalized
constraints = []

for i in range(n - 1):
    word1 = words[i]
    word2 = words[i + 1]
    min_len = min(len(word1), len(word2))
    
    for j in range(min_len):
        if word1[j] != word2[j]:
            if word1[j] > word2[j]:
                constraints.append((word1[j], word2[j]))
            break

# For each constraint (a,b), at least one of a or b must be capitalized
# We will choose one arbitrarily
capitals = set()

for a, b in constraints:
    if a not in capitals and b not in capitals:
        capitals.add(a)

print("Yes")
print(len(capitals))
if capitals:
    print(*sorted(capitals))
else:
    print()
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You all know that the Library of Bookland is the largest library in the world. There are dozens of thousands of books in the library.

Some long and uninteresting story was removed...

The alphabet of Bookland is so large that its letters are denoted by positive integers. Each letter can be small or large, the large version of a letter x is denoted by x'. BSCII encoding, which is used everywhere in Bookland, is made in that way so that large letters are presented in the order of the numbers they are denoted by, and small letters are presented in the order of the numbers they are denoted by, but all large letters are before all small letters. For example, the following conditions hold: 2 < 3, 2' < 3', 3' < 2.

A word x1, x2, ..., xa is not lexicographically greater than y1, y2, ..., yb if one of the two following conditions holds: 

  * a ≤ b and x1 = y1, ..., xa = ya, i.e. the first word is the prefix of the second word; 
  * there is a position 1 ≤ j ≤ min(a, b), such that x1 = y1, ..., xj - 1 = yj - 1 and xj < yj, i.e. at the first position where the words differ the first word has a smaller letter than the second word has. 



For example, the word "3' 7 5" is before the word "2 4' 6" in lexicographical order. It is said that sequence of words is in lexicographical order if each word is not lexicographically greater than the next word in the sequence.

Denis has a sequence of words consisting of small letters only. He wants to change some letters to large (let's call this process a capitalization) in such a way that the sequence of words is in lexicographical order. However, he soon realized that for some reason he can't change a single letter in a single word. He only can choose a letter and change all of its occurrences in all words to large letters. He can perform this operation any number of times with arbitrary letters of Bookland's alphabet.

Help Denis to choose which letters he needs to capitalize (make large) in order to make the sequence of words lexicographically ordered, or determine that it is impossible.

Note that some words can be equal.

Input

The first line contains two integers n and m (2 ≤ n ≤ 100 000, 1 ≤ m ≤ 100 000) — the number of words and the number of letters in Bookland's alphabet, respectively. The letters of Bookland's alphabet are denoted by integers from 1 to m.

Each of the next n lines contains a description of one word in format li, si, 1, si, 2, ..., si, li (1 ≤ li ≤ 100 000, 1 ≤ si, j ≤ m), where li is the length of the word, and si, j is the sequence of letters in the word. The words are given in the order Denis has them in the sequence.

It is guaranteed that the total length of all words is not greater than 100 000.

Output

In the first line print "Yes" (without quotes), if it is possible to capitalize some set of letters in such a way that the sequence of words becomes lexicographically ordered. Otherwise, print "No" (without quotes).

If the required is possible, in the second line print k — the number of letters Denis has to capitalize (make large), and in the third line print k distinct integers — these letters. Note that you don't need to minimize the value k.

You can print the letters in any order. If there are multiple answers, print any of them.

Examples

Input

4 3
1 2
1 1
3 1 3 2
2 1 1


Output

Yes
2
2 3 

Input

6 5
2 1 2
2 1 2
3 1 2 3
2 1 5
2 4 4
2 4 4


Output

Yes
0


Input

4 3
4 3 2 2 1
3 1 1 3
3 2 3 3
2 3 1


Output

No

Note

In the first example after Denis makes letters 2 and 3 large, the sequence looks like the following:

  * 2'
  * 1
  * 1 3' 2'
  * 1 1



The condition 2' < 1 holds, so the first word is not lexicographically larger than the second word. The second word is the prefix of the third word, so the are in lexicographical order. As the first letters of the third and the fourth words are the same, and 3' < 1, then the third word is not lexicographically larger than the fourth word.

In the second example the words are in lexicographical order from the beginning, so Denis can do nothing.

In the third example there is no set of letters such that if Denis capitalizes them, the sequence becomes lexicographically ordered.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a sequence of words made of letters denoted by positive integers
2. In Bookland's BSCII encoding:
   - Large letters (x') come before small letters (x)
   - Among large letters: smaller numbers come first (2' < 3')
   - Among small letters: smaller numbers come first (2 < 3)
   - But large letters come before small letters (2' < 2)

3. We can only capitalize entire letters - changing all occurrences of a letter to large form
4. We need to determine if we can make the sequence lexicographically ordered by capitalizing some letters

## Key Insights

1. When we capitalize a letter, all its occurrences in all words become large (and thus come before their small counterparts)
2. For two consecutive words to be in lexicographic order:
   - Either the first word is a prefix of the second word
   - Or at the first differing position, the letter in the first word is less than the letter in the second word (considering the capitalization)

3. The constraint is that we can only capitalize entire letters, not individual occurrences.

## Algorithm Approach

1. We need to check if there's a consistent way to assign "capitalized" status to letters such that the sequence is lexicographically ordered.

2. For each pair of consecutive words, we need to determine what constraints this imposes on the capitalization of letters.

3. For each pair of consecutive words (word i and word i+1):
   - If word i is a prefix of word i+1 → no constraint
   - Otherwise, at the first position where they differ, we need the letter in word i to be "less than" the letter in word i+1

4. The comparison depends on whether each letter is capitalized or not:
   - If a letter is capitalized, it becomes large and comes before all small versions
   - If a letter is not capitalized, it's small and follows the standard ordering

5. For two letters x and y:
   - If x is capitalized and y is not: x < y (since x' comes before y)
   - If x is not capitalized and y is capitalized: x > y (since x comes after x' in the ordering)
   - If both capitalized: x' < y' if x < y
   - If both not capitalized: x < y if x < y

6. We can model this as a constraint satisfaction problem and use a greedy approach:
   - Initialize all letters as not capitalized
   - For each consecutive pair of words, check the constraints
   - If a constraint is violated, try to fix it by capitalizing certain letters
   - Use a constraint propagation approach

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    words = []
    for _ in range(n):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        length = line[0]
        word = line[1:]
        words.append(word)
    
    # For each pair of consecutive words, we need to ensure ordering
    # We'll build constraints on which letters should be capitalized
    
    # Track which letters we decide to capitalize
    capitalized = [False] * (m + 1)  # 1-indexed
    
    # Process each pair of consecutive words
    for i in range(n - 1):
        word1 = words[i]
        word2 = words[i + 1]
        
        # Find the first position where they differ
        min_len = min(len(word1), len(word2))
        found_diff = False
        
        for pos in range(min_len):
            if word1[pos] != word2[pos]:
                letter1 = word1[pos]
                letter2 = word2[pos]
                found_diff = True
                
                # Check if current capitalization satisfies letter1 < letter2
                # We need to determine if letter1 < letter2 given our current capitalization
                
                # Case 1: both letters capitalized
                if capitalized[letter1] and capitalized[letter2]:
                    # Both are large: we need letter1 < letter2 (since x' < y' if x < y)
                    if letter1 >= letter2:
                        # This violates the constraint - we need to fix it
                        # We can't change the letters, only capitalize them
                        # So we need to make letter1' < letter2' which means letter1 < letter2
                        # But we have letter1 >= letter2, so this is impossible
                        print("No")
                        return
                
                # Case 2: only letter1 capitalized
                elif capitalized[letter1] and not capitalized[letter2]:
                    # letter1 is large, letter2 is small: letter1' < letter2
                    # This means x' < y where x >= y, but x' comes before all small versions
                    # So x' < y is always true only if x is some letter and y is another
                    # Actually, x' comes before y for any y, so this is always true
                    # But we need to check if x' < y (which is true by BSCII rule)
                    # So x' < y is true for any x and y
                    # So this case is always valid
                    pass
                
                # Case 3: only letter2 capitalized
                elif not capitalized[letter1] and capitalized[letter2]:
                    # letter1 is small, letter2 is large: letter1 < letter2'
                    # This means x < x' for some x, but x' comes before x, so x > x'
                    # So letter1 (small) > letter2' (large), which means letter1 is not < letter2'
                    # This violates the constraint
                    print("No")
                    return
                
                # Case 4: both not capitalized
                else:
                    # Both are small: we need letter1 < letter2
                    if letter1 >= letter2:
                        print("No")
                        return
                
                # If we reach here, the constraint is satisfied for this position
                break
        
        # If no difference found and word1 is prefix of word2, it's valid
        if not found_diff and len(word1) > len(word2):
            print("No")
            return
    
    # If we've processed all pairs without contradiction, we have a solution
    # But we might have multiple valid capitalizations - we need to check if our current
    # capitalization is valid or if we can adjust it
    
    # Actually, we need to be more careful - the above approach has a flaw
    
    # Let's reconstruct with a different approach:
    # We'll try to derive constraints on which letters must be capitalized
    
    # Reset and use a constraint-based approach
    capitalization = [False] * (m + 1)
    
    # For each consecutive pair, determine what constraints exist
    for i in range(n - 1):
        w1 = words[i]
        w2 = words[i + 1]
        
        min_len = min(len(w1), len(w2))
        
        # Check for prefix condition
        if len(w1) > len(w2):
            # w1 is longer than w2 and they match in prefix - impossible
            print("No")
            return
            
        # Find first difference position
        diff_pos = -1
        for pos in range(min_len):
            if w1[pos] != w2[pos]:
                diff_pos = pos
                break
        
        if diff_pos == -1:
            # Words are equal in prefix - check if w1 is prefix of w2
            if len(w1) > len(w2):
                print("No")
                return
            # Otherwise, they are equal or w1 is prefix of w2 - valid
            continue
        
        # We have a difference at position diff_pos
        l1 = w1[diff_pos]
        l2 = w2[diff_pos]
        
        # Now we need to ensure that l1 < l2 in the final ordering
        # This depends on whether l1 and l2 are capitalized
        
        # Case 1: both capitalized
        if capitalization[l1] and capitalization[l2]:
            if l1 >= l2:
                # l1' should be < l2', which requires l1 < l2
                # If l1 >= l2, this is impossible
                print("No")
                return
        
        # Case 2: only l1 capitalized
        elif capitalization[l1] and not capitalization[l2]:
            # l1' is large, l2 is small
            # l1' comes before all small letters (including l2)
            # So l1' < l2 is true
            # This satisfies the constraint
            pass
        
        # Case 3: only l2 capitalized
        elif not capitalization[l1] and capitalization[l2]:
            # l1 is small, l2' is large
            # l1 (small) comes after l2' (large) in ordering
            # So l1 > l2' which means l1 is not less than l2
            # This violates the constraint
            print("No")
            return
        
        # Case 4: both not capitalized
        else:
            if l1 >= l2:
                print("No")
                return
    
    # If we get here, we have a valid capitalization
    # But our current capitalization might not be optimal
    # We need to ensure we have a consistent assignment
    
    # Actually, let's try a different approach - we can make any letter capitalized
    # and check if it leads to a valid ordering
    
    # Since the problem asks for any valid answer, we can try to find one
    # and we already have constraints from the pairs
    
    # Let's modify our approach to build constraints on capitalization
    
    # For each pair, we have constraints on what capitalization assignments work
    # We'll build a graph of constraints
    
    # But given the complexity, let's implement a simpler but correct approach
    
    # Try all possible capitalizations? Too expensive
    
    # Alternative: greedy approach - start with no capitalization, then fix violations
    capitalization = [False] * (m + 1)
    
    valid = True
    for i in range(n - 1):
        w1 = words[i]
        w2 = words[i + 1]
        
        min_len = min(len(w1), len(w2))
        diff_pos = -1
        
        for pos in range(min_len):
            if w1[pos] != w2[pos]:
                diff_pos = pos
                break
        
        if diff_pos == -1:
            # One is prefix of other
            if len(w1) > len(w2):
                valid = False
                break
            continue
        
        l1 = w1[diff_pos]
        l2 = w2[diff_pos]
        
        # Check if current capitalization satisfies l1 < l2
        if capitalization[l1] and capitalization[l2]:
            if l1 >= l2:
                valid = False
                break
        elif not capitalization[l1] and capitalization[l2]:
            # l1 is small, l2 is large: l1 > l2' (since small > large)
            valid = False
            break
        elif not capitalization[l1] and not capitalization[l2]:
            if l1 >= l2:
                valid = False
                break
    
    if not valid:
        print("No")
        return
    
    # If we reach here, the no-capitalization assignment works
    # But we might be able to add more capitalizations?
    # Actually, adding capitalizations might create new constraints
    
    # We need to check if there's a consistent capitalization that works
    # The problem is that adding a capitalization might violate constraints
    
    # Let's try a different constraint-based approach
    
    # Build constraints: for each pair, we know what capitalization combinations are valid
    
    # Instead, let's try to find if there exists any capitalization that works
    # We can use the fact that if no capitalization is needed, it works
    
    # We'll build a system of constraints and check feasibility
    
    # But given time, let's implement a simpler but correct solution based on the examples
    
    # After reviewing, I realize my approach has flaws
    
    # Let me try a different, more systematic approach:
    
    def works(capitalized):
        for i in range(n - 1):
            w1 = words[i]
            w2 = words[i + 1]
            
            min_len = min(len(w1), len(w2))
            diff_pos = -1
            
            for pos in range(min_len):
                if w1[pos] != w2[pos]:
                    diff_pos = pos
                    break
            
            if diff_pos == -1:
                if len(w1) > len(w2):
                    return False
                continue
            
            l1 = w1[diff_pos]
            l2 = w2[diff_pos]
            
            # Check if l1 < l2 given the capitalization
            if capitalized[l1] and capitalized[l2]:
                # both large: need l1 < l2
                if l1 >= l2:
                    return False
            elif not capitalized[l1] and capitalized[l2]:
                # l1 small, l2 large: small > large, so l1 > l2' -> not valid
                return False
            elif not capitalized[l1] and not capitalized[l2]:
                # both small: need l1 < l2
                if l1 >= l2:
                    return False
        
        return True
    
    # Try to find a valid capitalization
    # Since we need to output any valid one, we can try to find one that works
    
    # The empty capitalization always works if no constraints are violated
    # Check if empty capitalization works
    if works([False] * (m + 1)):
        print("Yes")
        print(0)
    else:
        # Try adding one letter at a time
        # But this might be exponential
        
        # Instead, let's think about the constraints
        # For each pair, we have constraints on letter pairs
        
        # Actually, let's go back to the initial insight
        
        # After careful thought, I realize that the capitalization decision for each letter 
        # should be determined by the constraints from the pairs
        
        # For each pair, if we have a conflict, we need to resolve it
        
        # Let me implement a simpler version that handles the examples correctly
        
        # From the examples:
        # In example 1: we need to capitalize 2 and 3
        # In example 2: no capitalization needed
        # In example 3: impossible
        
        # Re-examine example 3:
        # 4 3 2 2 1
        # 3 1 1 3
        # 3 2 3 3
        # 2 3 1
        
        # Compare word0 and word1:
        # pos0: 4 vs 1 -> different, need 4 < 1 with capitalization
        # If 4 is capitalized and 1 is not: 4' < 1 -> impossible (4' comes before 1, so 4' < 1 is true)
        # If 4 is not capitalized and 1 is capitalized: 4 < 1' -> false since 4 > 1
        # If both capitalized: 4' < 1' -> false since 4 > 1
        # If both not: 4 < 1 -> false
        # So impossible
        
        # So the constraint is: for a difference at position pos with letters a and b, 
        # we need a < b, and this can be achieved through capitalization only if 
        # a is capitalized and b is not (then a' < b), or both not and a < b
        
        # Actually, if a is capitalized and b is not: a' < b is true (because a' comes before all small)
        # If a is not capitalized and b is capitalized: a < b' is false because b' comes before a in ordering
        # Wait, no: in BSCII, large letters come before small letters
        # So x' < y for any y
        # So x' < y is true
        # So if a is capitalized and b is not: a' < b is true
        # If a is not capitalized and b is capitalized: a < b' is false because b' comes before a in ordering
        # So a is after b', so a > b'
        
        # Correction:
        # If a is capitalized and b is not: a' < b (since a' comes before b)
        # If a is not capitalized and b is capitalized: a < b' (but b' comes before a in ordering, so a > b')
        # So a is not < b'
        
        # Therefore, the only valid cases are:
        # - a < b and both not capitalized
        # - a is capitalized and b is not capitalized
        
        # So for a letter pair (a,b) where a > b, it's impossible to have a < b
        
        # Let me implement this:
        
        possible = True
        # We'll build a constraint that for each position where a > b, if a is capitalized and b is not, we get a' < b, which is true
        # But we need to ensure that in all cases we have the ordering

        for i in range(n - 1):

            w2 = words[i + 1]
            min_len = min(len(w1), len(w2))
            
            for pos in range(min_len):
                if w1[pos] != w2[pos]:
                    a = w1[pos]
                    b = w2[pos]

                    # This can happen in two cases:
                    # 1. a < b and both are not capitalized
                    # 2. a is capitalized and b is not capitalized
                    
                    # Case 1: both not capitalized
                    if a < b:
                        # This is valid with no capitalization
                        pass

                    # But we can't make a capitalized if it creates a problem elsewhere

                    # The only possibility is a' < b, which requires a to be capitalized and b not capitalized
                    # So if a >= b, we need to capitalize a and not capitalize b

                    # If we capitalize a and capitalize b, we have a' and b': a' < b' only if a < b, which is false if a >= b
                    # If we capitalize a and not capitalize b: a' < b is true
                    # If we not capitalize a and capitalize b: a < b' is false (since b' comes before a)
                    
                    # So if a >= b, we must have a capitalized and b not capitalized
                    
                    if a >= b:
                        # We need to ensure that a is capitalized and b is not capitalized
                        # But this might conflict with other constraints
                        
                        # For now, we just mark that this constraint exists
                        # We'll try to find a valid capitalization later
                        pass
            
            # Check prefix condition
            if len(w1) > len(w2):
                possible = False
                break
        
        if not possible:
            print("No")
            return
        
        # Now we need to build a capitalization that satisfies all constraints
        # Specifically, for each pair where a >= b, we need a capitalized and b not capitalized
        
        capitalization = [False] * (m + 1)
        
        # Collect all constraints
        constraints = []
        
        for i in range(n - 1):
            w1 = words[i]
            w2 = words[i + 1]
            min_len = min(len(w1), len(w2))
            
            for pos in range(min_len):
                if w1[pos] != w2[pos]:
                    a = w1[pos]
                    b = w2[pos]
                    
                    if a >= b:
                        constraints.append((a, b))  # need a capitalized, b not capitalized
        
        # Try to build a capitalization that satisfies all constraints
        # For each constraint (a, b), we need a in capitalization, b not in capitalization
        
        # This is a constraint satisfaction problem - we need to ensure that no letter is both in and not in
        
        # Initially, make all letters not capitalized
        capitalization = [False] * (m + 1)
        
        # Apply constraints
        for a, b in constraints:
            capitalization[a] = True
            # We don't need to do anything about b - we'll ensure it's not capitalized later if needed
        
        # Now verify if this capitalization works
        valid = True
        for i in range(n - 1):
            w1 = words[i]
            w2 = words[i + 1]
            min_len = min(len(w1), len(w2))
            
            for pos in range(min_len):
                if w1[pos] != w2[pos]:
                    a = w1[pos]
                    b = w2[pos]
                    
                    if capitalization[a] and capitalization[b]:
                        # both capitalized: need a < b
                        if a >= b:
                            valid = False
                            break
                    elif not capitalization[a] and capitalization[b]:
                        # a small, b large: a > b' (since b' comes before a)
                        # so a is not < b'
                        valid = False
                        break
                    elif not capitalization[a] and not capitalization[b]:
                        # both small: need a < b
                        if a >= b:
                            valid = False
                            break
            if not valid:
                break
        
        if valid:
            print("Yes")
            capitalization_list = [i for i in range(1, m + 1) if capitalization[i]]
            print(len(capitalization_list))
            if capitalization_list:
                print(*capitalization_list)
            else:
                print()
        else:
            print("No")
```

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    words = []
    for _ in range(n):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        length = line[0]
        word = line[1:]
        words.append(word)
    
    # For each consecutive pair of words, we need to ensure the first is <= the second
    # We'll build a set of constraints: for each position where they differ, 
    # we need to determine what capitalization makes the first letter < the second
    
    # The key insight: 
    # - If letter a appears in the first word and letter b in the second at a difference position,
    #   and a >= b, then we must capitalize a and not capitalize b (so a' < b)
    # - If a < b, then we can either have both not capitalized (a < b) or a capitalized and b not capitalized (a' < b)
    # - We cannot have a not capitalized and b capitalized (because then a < b' is false, as b' comes before a)
    
    # So we need to ensure that for every constraint where a >= b, we have a capitalized and b not capitalized
    
    # Build constraints: list of (a, b) where a >= b and we need a' < b
    constraints = []
    
    for i in range(n - 1):
        w1 = words[i]
        w2 = words[i + 1]
        
        min_len = min(len(w1), len(w2))
        for pos in range(min_len):
            if w1[pos] != w2[pos]:
                a = w1[pos]
                b = w2[pos]
                
                # If a >= b, we need a to be capitalized and b not to be capitalized
                if a >= b:
                    constraints.append((a, b))
                break  # Only first difference matters
        
        # If w1 is longer than w2 and they match in prefix, it's invalid
        if len(w1) > len(w2):
            print("No")
            return
    
    # Now we have constraints: for each (a,b) in constraints, we need a capitalized and b not capitalized
    # But we also need to check consistency - if we require a capitalized and b not capitalized,
    # and also require a not capitalized and c capitalized, then conflict
    
    # Build the set of letters that must be capitalized
    must_capitalize = set()
    must_not_capitalize = set()
    
    for a, b in constraints:
        must_capitalize.add(a)
        must_not_capitalize.add(b)
    
    # Now check for conflicts: if a letter is in both must_capitalize and must_not_capitalize
    # This would be a contradiction
    for letter in must_capitalize:
        if letter in must_not_capitalize:
            print("No")
            return
    
    # Additionally, we need to verify that with this capitalization, the sequence is indeed lexicographically ordered
    capitalization = [False] * (m + 1)
    for letter in must_capitalize:
        capitalization[letter] = True
    
    # Verify for each consecutive pair
    valid = True
    for i in range(n - 1):
        w1 = words[i]
        w2 = words[i + 1]

                a = w1[pos]
                b = w2[pos]
                
                # Check if a < b with current capitalization

                    if a >= b:
                        valid = False
                        break

                    valid = False
                    break

                    # both small: need a < b
                    if a >= b:
                        valid = False
                        break
        if not valid:
            break
```
